========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hello, Doctor. Well... I suppose I came in because... well, it’s my mind. My mind just will not hold onto things anymore, and it’s become quite frightening, to be perfectly honest with you. I’ve always been a sharp person, got my degree, always prided myself on being capable and organized. Even now that I'm not working, I try to stay involved, keep myself useful. I help out with this youth maker program in the community—it’s a good group, we teach the kids how to build things, work with their hands, do a bit of basic engineering. 

But... I sat down the other day to write a simple note for the program. Just a basic outline, some measurements and materials for the kids. It's something I could do in my sleep before. I mean, I’ve done it a thousand times over the years without a second thought. But I sat there at my desk, pen in hand, and... the numbers and the words, they just scattered on me. They completely scattered. I couldn't hold the thought long enough to write it down. I just stared at the paper for I don't know how long, trying to force it to make sense, but it was like trying to catch water with my bare hands. It takes me forever to do the simplest things now because my brain just refuses to cooperate, and I just... I don't know what's happening to me.

Memory? No... no, I wouldn't say it's my memory. And I'm certainly not confused about where I am or who people are. I mean, I know what day it is, I know I'm sitting here in your office. I know my neighbors' names, I know all the kids at the maker program. It's not like that at all. It's not like I'm losing my past or forgetting where I put my keys. 

If you asked me right now what the exact dimensions of the birdhouses we build are, I could tell you without missing a beat—six inches by eight inches, quarter-inch plywood, assembled with brad nails. The information is all still in there. My memory is perfectly intact, I assure you. 

It's... well, it's like I was saying before, it's the execution of it. The filing cabinet in my head is still perfectly organized, but it’s as if the drawer is jammed shut and I'm just pulling and pulling at the handle. I remember what I want to do, I know exactly what I'm trying to achieve, but my brain just won't process the steps to get there. So, no, I'm not forgetting things, and I'm not lost in time. I'm very much aware of my surroundings, perhaps too aware of how useless my brain is being right now. It just refuses to process the thoughts, even though the memories themselves are sitting right there, clear as day.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Finding words? No, no, I wouldn't say that at all. My vocabulary is... well, it's as robust as it ever was. When I'm actually in a conversation, the words come out just fine. 

For instance, just last Tuesday, or maybe it was Wednesday—no, it was Tuesday, because that's when the lumber delivery comes in for the youth program—I was having quite a long chat with the delivery driver about the quality of the pine they've been sending us lately. It’s been awfully knotty, you see, and you can’t have the kids trying to drive brad nails through knots, it’s just asking for a splinter or a bent nail, and it ruins the whole birdhouse. Anyway, I was explaining all of this to him, quite clearly, articulating exactly what we needed for the next batch. I didn't stumble over my words, I didn't forget what a two-by-four was called, I didn't have to pause and search for the right terminology. 

And when he was explaining the supply chain issues they’ve been having at the mill, I understood him perfectly. I followed his whole explanation about the timber shortages and the shipping delays without any trouble whatsoever. Even with the kids at the program—and let me tell you, the slang they use these days is practically a foreign language—but I can still follow along, I understand what they're trying to communicate to me. 

So, no, communicating with people, understanding them... that's not the problem. I can hold a conversation just fine, as you can probably tell. If you and I are sitting here talking, or if I'm listening to you, I understand every single word you're saying. The language part of my brain seems to be doing its job properly. It's just... it's when I'm left to my own devices, trying to organize my own thoughts into an action, like with that piece of paper I mentioned earlier, that everything just grinds to an absolute halt. But the talking and the listening? No, those are perfectly fine.

I remember what I want to do, I know exactly what I'm trying to achieve, but my brain just won't process the steps to get there. So, no, I'm not forgetting things, and I'm not lost in time. I'm very much aware of my surroundings, perhaps too aware of how useless my brain is being right now. It just refuses to process the thoughts, even though the memories themselves are sitting right there, clear as day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble finding the right words when you speak, or difficulty understanding what others are saying to you?\n\nPatient: Finding words? When I'm actually in a conversation, the words come out just fine. \n\nFor instance, just last Tuesday, or maybe it was Wednesday—no, it was Tuesday, because that's when the lumber delivery comes in for the youth program—I was having quite a long chat with the delivery driver about the quality of the pine they've been sending us lately. It’s been awfully knotty, you see, and you can’t have the kids trying to drive brad nails through knots, it’s just asking for a splinter or a bent nail, and it ruins the whole birdhouse. Anyway, I was explaining all of this to him, quite clearly, articulating exactly what we needed for the next batch. I didn't stumble over my words, I didn't forget what a two-by-four was called, I didn't have to pause and search for the right terminology. \n\nAnd when he was explaining the supply chain issues they’ve been having at the mill, I understood him perfectly. I followed his whole explanation about the timber shortages and the shipping delays without any trouble whatsoever. Even with the kids at the program—and let me tell you, the slang they use these days is practically a foreign language—but I can still follow along, I understand what they're trying to communicate to me. \n\nSo, no, communicating with people, understanding them... that's not the problem. I can hold a conversation just fine, as you can probably tell. If you and I are sitting here talking, or if I'm listening to you, I understand every single word you're saying. The language part of my brain seems to be doing its job properly. It's just... it's when I'm left to my own devices, trying to organize my own thoughts into an action, like with that piece of paper I mentioned earlier, that everything just grinds to an absolute halt. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Coordination? Oh, no, not at all. My hands work just as well as they always have. I mean, if my physical coordination were failing me, I certainly wouldn't be anywhere near a band saw or a drill press, let alone teaching twelve-year-olds how to use them safely. 

Just Thursday—yes, Thursday afternoon when we had the shop open—I was showing one of the boys, young Tommy, how to properly hold a coping saw to get a nice, clean curve on a piece of wood. My hands were perfectly steady. I can still thread a tiny little set screw into a brass hinge without dropping it, and I can tie my boots every morning in a firm double knot just like I've done for fifty years. I've always been good with my hands, you see. I built my own back deck back in '98, did all the measurements, all the cuts myself, and even now, I can still swing a hammer and hit the nail dead on the head every single time. 

And the writing... you mentioned writing by hand. See, that's the truly frustrating part of all this! The physical act of holding the pen, the grip, the way my hand physically moves across the paper—that's all perfectly fine. My handwriting is still as neat and legible as it was when I took drafting classes in college. It's not my hand that's the problem, it's... well, it's the brain telling the hand *what* words to form. If you asked me right now to just draw a straight line, or copy a shape, or even trace a stencil, I could do it with absolute precision. My motor skills haven't deteriorated one bit. 

It's really quite strange when you think about it. My body, my hands, my physical coordination... they are all perfectly willing and able to do the work. It's just that the engine driving them—the mind—has completely stalled out on me. So, no, using tools, tying shoes, the mechanics of writing... none of that feels awkward at all. It's just the thinking behind it that's gone completely sideways.

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. I'm Dr. [Your Name]. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Doctor. Well... I suppose I came in because... well, it’s my mind. My mind just will not hold onto things anymore, and it’s become quite frightening, to be perfectly honest with you. I’ve always been a sharp person, got my degree, always prided myself on being capable and organized. Even now that I'm not working, I try to stay involved, keep myself useful. I help out with this youth maker program in the community—it’s a good group, we teach the kids how to build things, work with their hands, do a bit of basic engineering. \n\nBut... I sat down the other day to write a simple note for the program. Just a basic outline, some measurements and materials for the kids. It's something I could do in my sleep before. I mean, I’ve done it a thousand times over the years without a second thought. But I sat there at my desk, pen in hand, and... the numbers and the words, they just scattered on me. They completely scattered. I couldn't hold the thought long enough to write it down. I just stared at the paper for I don't know how long, trying to force it to make sense, but it was like trying to catch water with my bare hands. It takes me forever to do the simplest things now because my brain just refuses to cooperate, and I just... I don't know what's happening to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble remembering things, or do you ever feel confused about time, place, or people around you?\n\nPatient: Memory? No... no, I wouldn't say it's my memory. And I'm certainly not confused about where I am or who people are. I mean, I know what day it is, I know I'm sitting here in your office. I know my neighbors' names, I know all the kids at the maker program. It's not like that at all. It's not like I'm losing my past or forgetting where I put my keys. \n\nIf you asked me right now what the exact dimensions of the birdhouses we build are, I could tell you without missing a beat—six inches by eight inches, quarter-inch plywood, assembled with brad nails. The information is all still in there. My memory is perfectly intact, I assure you. \n\nIt's... well, it's like I was saying before, it's the execution of it. The filing cabinet in my head is still perfectly organized, but it’s as if the drawer is jammed shut and I'm just pulling and pulling at the handle. I remember what I want to do, I know exactly what I'm trying to achieve, but my brain just won't process the steps to get there. So, no, I'm not forgetting things, and I'm not lost in time. I'm very much aware of my surroundings, perhaps too aware of how useless my brain is being right now. It just refuses to process the thoughts, even though the memories themselves are sitting right there, clear as day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble finding the right words when you speak, or difficulty understanding what others are saying to you?\n\nPatient: Finding words? No, no, I wouldn't say that at all. My vocabulary is... well, it's as robust as it ever was. When I'm actually in a conversation, the words come out just fine. \n\nFor instance, just last Tuesday, or maybe it was Wednesday—no, it was Tuesday, because that's when the lumber delivery comes in for the youth program—I was having quite a long chat with the delivery driver about the quality of the pine they've been sending us lately. It’s been awfully knotty, you see, and you can’t have the kids trying to drive brad nails through knots, it’s just asking for a splinter or a bent nail, and it ruins the whole birdhouse. Anyway, I was explaining all of this to him, quite clearly, articulating exactly what we needed for the next batch. I didn't stumble over my words, I didn't forget what a two-by-four was called, I didn't have to pause and search for the right terminology. \n\nAnd when he was explaining the supply chain issues they’ve been having at the mill, I understood him perfectly. I followed his whole explanation about the timber shortages and the shipping delays without any trouble whatsoever. Even with the kids at the program—and let me tell you, the slang they use these days is practically a foreign language—but I can still follow along, I understand what they're trying to communicate to me. \n\nSo, no, communicating with people, understanding them... that's not the problem. I can hold a conversation just fine, as you can probably tell. If you and I are sitting here talking, or if I'm listening to you, I understand every single word you're saying. The language part of my brain seems to be doing its job properly. It's just... it's when I'm left to my own devices, trying to organize my own thoughts into an action, like with that piece of paper I mentioned earlier, that everything just grinds to an absolute halt. But the talking and the listening? No, those are perfectly fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble performing tasks that require coordination, like tying your shoes, using tools, or writing by hand—things you’ve done many times before but now feel awkward or difficult to carry out?\n\nPatient: Coordination? Oh, no, not at all. My hands work just as well as they always have. I mean, if my physical coordination were failing me, I certainly wouldn't be anywhere near a band saw or a drill press, let alone teaching twelve-year-olds how to use them safely. \n\nJust Thursday—yes, Thursday afternoon when we had the shop open—I was showing one of the boys, young Tommy, how to properly hold a coping saw to get a nice, clean curve on a piece of wood. My hands were perfectly steady. I can still thread a tiny little set screw into a brass hinge without dropping it, and I can tie my boots every morning in a firm double knot just like I've done for fifty years. I've always been good with my hands, you see. I built my own back deck back in '98, did all the measurements, all the cuts myself, and even now, I can still swing a hammer and hit the nail dead on the head every single time. \n\nAnd the writing... you mentioned writing by hand. See, that's the truly frustrating part of all this! The physical act of holding the pen, the grip, the way my hand physically moves across the paper—that's all perfectly fine. My handwriting is still as neat and legible as it was when I took drafting classes in college. It's not my hand that's the problem, it's... well, it's the brain telling the hand *what* words to form. If you asked me right now to just draw a straight line, or copy a shape, or even trace a stencil, I could do it with absolute precision. My motor skills haven't deteriorated one bit. \n\nIt's really quite strange when you think about it. My body, my hands, my physical coordination... they are all perfectly willing and able to do the work. It's just that the engine driving them—the mind—has completely stalled out on me. So, no, using tools, tying shoes, the mechanics of writing... none of that feels awkward at all. It's just the thinking behind it that's gone completely sideways.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, exactly. That... that is exactly it. It's the planning, the organizing of the thoughts. That's actually the whole reason I came in to see you in the first place. I just... my mind simply will not hold onto things anymore. 

Like I was trying to explain earlier with that piece of paper... I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program. Just a basic list of materials we need for the next project, or a schedule for the kids, something I used to be able to do in my sleep. I've done it a thousand times over the years. I know we need the plywood, I know we need the brad nails, I know we need the wood glue. But when I actually sit down at my desk, with the pen in my hand—and as I said, the hand works fine—but when I try to actually put the plan together on the page... it's like the numbers and the words just scatter on me. 

I'll think, "Okay, we need three sheets of quarter-inch plywood," and then I'll try to think about how many boxes of nails that requires, and suddenly the plywood thought is gone. It just vanishes. And then I'm sitting there staring at a blank piece of paper, trying to wrangle these thoughts back into order, but they just won't line up. It's like trying to hold water in your hands. You know it's there, you can feel it, but it just slips right through your fingers no matter how tightly you squeeze them together. 

I used to be able to juggle three different projects at once in the shop. I could have one group of kids sanding, another group measuring, and another group assembling, and I knew exactly where everyone was and what they were doing at any given moment. I could keep track of it all in my head without even breaking a sweat. But now? Now, just trying to figure out the steps to build a single birdhouse—a project I designed myself, mind you—it feels like trying to solve a complex algebraic equation in my head while someone is shouting at me. The individual tasks, like the cutting or the gluing, those are familiar, yes. But trying to string them together into a coherent plan? It's just... it's impossible. My brain just won't do it anymore. It just scatters everything to the wind.

No... no, I wouldn't say it's my memory. And I'm certainly not confused about where I am or who people are. I mean, I know what day it is, I know I'm sitting here in your office. I know my neighbors' names, I know all the kids at the maker program. It's not like that at all. It's not like I'm losing my past or forgetting where I put my keys. \n\nIf you asked me right now what the exact dimensions of the birdhouses we build are, I could tell you without missing a beat—six inches by eight inches, quarter-inch plywood, assembled with brad nails. The information is all still in there. My memory is perfectly intact, I assure you. \n\nIt's... well, it's like I was saying before, it's the execution of it. The filing cabinet in my head is still perfectly organized, but it’s as if the drawer is jammed shut and I'm just pulling and pulling at the handle. I remember what I want to do, I know exactly what I'm trying to achieve, but my brain just won't process the steps to get there. So, no, I'm not forgetting things, and I'm not lost in time. I'm very much aware of my surroundings, perhaps too aware of how useless my brain is being right now. It just refuses to process the thoughts, even though the memories themselves are sitting right there, clear as day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble finding the right words when you speak, or difficulty understanding what others are saying to you?\n\nPatient: Finding words? No, no, I wouldn't say that at all. My vocabulary is... well, it's as robust as it ever was. When I'm actually in a conversation, the words come out just fine. \n\nFor instance, just last Tuesday, or maybe it was Wednesday—no, it was Tuesday, because that's when the lumber delivery comes in for the youth program—I was having quite a long chat with the delivery driver about the quality of the pine they've been sending us lately. It’s been awfully knotty, you see, and you can’t have the kids trying to drive brad nails through knots, it’s just asking for a splinter or a bent nail, and it ruins the whole birdhouse. Anyway, I was explaining all of this to him, quite clearly, articulating exactly what we needed for the next batch. I didn't stumble over my words, I didn't forget what a two-by-four was called, I didn't have to pause and search for the right terminology. \n\nAnd when he was explaining the supply chain issues they’ve been having at the mill, I understood him perfectly. I followed his whole explanation about the timber shortages and the shipping delays without any trouble whatsoever. Even with the kids at the program—and let me tell you, the slang they use these days is practically a foreign language—but I can still follow along, I understand what they're trying to communicate to me. \n\nSo, no, communicating with people, understanding them... that's not the problem. I can hold a conversation just fine, as you can probably tell. If you and I are sitting here talking, or if I'm listening to you, I understand every single word you're saying. The language part of my brain seems to be doing its job properly. It's just... it's when I'm left to my own devices, trying to organize my own thoughts into an action, like with that piece of paper I mentioned earlier, that everything just grinds to an absolute halt. But the talking and the listening? No, those are perfectly fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble performing tasks that require coordination, like tying your shoes, using tools, or writing by hand—things you’ve done many times before but now feel awkward or difficult to carry out?\n\nPatient: Coordination? Oh, no, not at all. My hands work just as well as they always have. I mean, if my physical coordination were failing me, I certainly wouldn't be anywhere near a band saw or a drill press, let alone teaching twelve-year-olds how to use them safely. \n\nJust Thursday—yes, Thursday afternoon when we had the shop open—I was showing one of the boys, young Tommy, how to properly hold a coping saw to get a nice, clean curve on a piece of wood. My hands were perfectly steady. I can still thread a tiny little set screw into a brass hinge without dropping it, and I can tie my boots every morning in a firm double knot just like I've done for fifty years. I've always been good with my hands, you see. I built my own back deck back in '98, did all the measurements, all the cuts myself, and even now, I can still swing a hammer and hit the nail dead on the head every single time. \n\nAnd the writing... you mentioned writing by hand. See, that's the truly frustrating part of all this! The physical act of holding the pen, the grip, the way my hand physically moves across the paper—that's all perfectly fine. My handwriting is still as neat and legible as it was when I took drafting classes in college. It's not my hand that's the problem, it's... well, it's the brain telling the hand *what* words to form. If you asked me right now to just draw a straight line, or copy a shape, or even trace a stencil, I could do it with absolute precision. My motor skills haven't deteriorated one bit. \n\nIt's really quite strange when you think about it. My body, my hands, my physical coordination... they are all perfectly willing and able to do the work. It's just that the engine driving them—the mind—has completely stalled out on me. So, no, using tools, tying shoes, the mechanics of writing... none of that feels awkward at all. It's just the thinking behind it that's gone completely sideways.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble planning out steps for a project or keeping track of multiple things at once, even if each individual task feels familiar?\n\nPatient: Yes, exactly. That... that is exactly it. It's the planning, the organizing of the thoughts. That's actually the whole reason I came in to see you in the first place. I just... my mind simply will not hold onto things anymore. \n\nLike I was trying to explain earlier with that piece of paper... I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program. Just a basic list of materials we need for the next project, or a schedule for the kids, something I used to be able to do in my sleep. I've done it a thousand times over the years. I know we need the plywood, I know we need the brad nails, I know we need the wood glue. But when I actually sit down at my desk, with the pen in my hand—and as I said, the hand works fine—but when I try to actually put the plan together on the page... it's like the numbers and the words just scatter on me. \n\nI'll think, \"Okay, we need three sheets of quarter-inch plywood,\" and then I'll try to think about how many boxes of nails that requires, and suddenly the plywood thought is gone. It just vanishes. And then I'm sitting there staring at a blank piece of paper, trying to wrangle these thoughts back into order, but they just won't line up. It's like trying to hold water in your hands. You know it's there, you can feel it, but it just slips right through your fingers no matter how tightly you squeeze them together. \n\nI used to be able to juggle three different projects at once in the shop. I could have one group of kids sanding, another group measuring, and another group assembling, and I knew exactly where everyone was and what they were doing at any given moment. I could keep track of it all in my head without even breaking a sweat. But now? Now, just trying to figure out the steps to build a single birdhouse—a project I designed myself, mind you—it feels like trying to solve a complex algebraic equation in my head while someone is shouting at me. The individual tasks, like the cutting or the gluing, those are familiar, yes. But trying to string them together into a coherent plan? It's just... it's impossible. My brain just won't do it anymore. It just scatters everything to the wind.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{
  "candidates": ["F06.7", "F02.80", "F04", "F01.50"],
  "note": "",
  "is_final": false
}

Anyway, I was explaining all of this to him, quite clearly, articulating exactly what we needed for the next batch. I didn't stumble over my words, I didn't forget what a two-by-four was called, I didn't have to pause and search for the right terminology. \n\nAnd when he was explaining the supply chain issues they’ve been having at the mill, I understood him perfectly. I followed his whole explanation about the timber shortages and the shipping delays without any trouble whatsoever. Even with the kids at the program—and let me tell you, the slang they use these days is practically a foreign language—but I can still follow along, I understand what they're trying to communicate to me. \n\nSo, no, communicating with people, understanding them... that's not the problem. I can hold a conversation just fine, as you can probably tell. If you and I are sitting here talking, or if I'm listening to you, I understand every single word you're saying. The language part of my brain seems to be doing its job properly. It's just... it's when I'm left to my own devices, trying to organize my own thoughts into an action, like with that piece of paper I mentioned earlier, that everything just grinds to an absolute halt. But the talking and the listening? No, those are perfectly fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble performing tasks that require coordination, like tying your shoes, using tools, or writing by hand—things you’ve done many times before but now feel awkward or difficult to carry out?\n\nPatient: Coordination? Oh, no, not at all. My hands work just as well as they always have. I mean, if my physical coordination were failing me, I certainly wouldn't be anywhere near a band saw or a drill press, let alone teaching twelve-year-olds how to use them safely. \n\nJust Thursday—yes, Thursday afternoon when we had the shop open—I was showing one of the boys, young Tommy, how to properly hold a coping saw to get a nice, clean curve on a piece of wood. My hands were perfectly steady. I can still thread a tiny little set screw into a brass hinge without dropping it, and I can tie my boots every morning in a firm double knot just like I've done for fifty years. I've always been good with my hands, you see. I built my own back deck back in '98, did all the measurements, all the cuts myself, and even now, I can still swing a hammer and hit the nail dead on the head every single time. \n\nAnd the writing... you mentioned writing by hand. See, that's the truly frustrating part of all this! The physical act of holding the pen, the grip, the way my hand physically moves across the paper—that's all perfectly fine. My handwriting is still as neat and legible as it was when I took drafting classes in college. It's not my hand that's the problem, it's... well, it's the brain telling the hand *what* words to form. If you asked me right now to just draw a straight line, or copy a shape, or even trace a stencil, I could do it with absolute precision. My motor skills haven't deteriorated one bit. \n\nIt's really quite strange when you think about it. My body, my hands, my physical coordination... they are all perfectly willing and able to do the work. It's just that the engine driving them—the mind—has completely stalled out on me. So, no, using tools, tying shoes, the mechanics of writing... none of that feels awkward at all. It's just the thinking behind it that's gone completely sideways.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble planning out steps for a project or keeping track of multiple things at once, even if each individual task feels familiar?\n\nPatient: Yes, exactly. That... that is exactly it. It's the planning, the organizing of the thoughts. That's actually the whole reason I came in to see you in the first place. I just... my mind simply will not hold onto things anymore. \n\nLike I was trying to explain earlier with that piece of paper... I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program. Just a basic list of materials we need for the next project, or a schedule for the kids, something I used to be able to do in my sleep. I've done it a thousand times over the years. I know we need the plywood, I know we need the brad nails, I know we need the wood glue. But when I actually sit down at my desk, with the pen in my hand—and as I said, the hand works fine—but when I try to actually put the plan together on the page... it's like the numbers and the words just scatter on me. \n\nI'll think, \"Okay, we need three sheets of quarter-inch plywood,\" and then I'll try to think about how many boxes of nails that requires, and suddenly the plywood thought is gone. It just vanishes. And then I'm sitting there staring at a blank piece of paper, trying to wrangle these thoughts back into order, but they just won't line up. It's like trying to hold water in your hands. You know it's there, you can feel it, but it just slips right through your fingers no matter how tightly you squeeze them together. \n\nI used to be able to juggle three different projects at once in the shop. I could have one group of kids sanding, another group measuring, and another group assembling, and I knew exactly where everyone was and what they were doing at any given moment. I could keep track of it all in my head without even breaking a sweat. But now? Now, just trying to figure out the steps to build a single birdhouse—a project I designed myself, mind you—it feels like trying to solve a complex algebraic equation in my head while someone is shouting at me. The individual tasks, like the cutting or the gluing, those are familiar, yes. But trying to string them together into a coherent plan? It's just... it's impossible. My brain just won't do it anymore. It just scatters everything to the wind.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you found it hard to start tasks or activities, even when you know you need to do them, or do you often feel stuck and unable to get going?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 67 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Starting them? No, no, it's not a matter of not wanting to do them or being unable to get out of the chair. I have the drive to get things done, I really do. The starting part is actually quite easy for me. 

Take yesterday morning, for example. I woke up at 6:00 AM sharp, same as I have every day since I retired. I had my coffee—black, just one cup, because Mary always used to say too much caffeine made me jittery, though I think it was just her excuse to keep the rest of the pot for herself. Anyway, I knew I needed to sort through the new donation bins for the youth center. We get all sorts of things dropped off by well-meaning folks, you know, old hand planes that are rusted completely shut, chisels with the handles rotted away, and someone has to go through them and decide what's salvageable for the kids and what just belongs in the scrap heap. 

I didn't sit there putting it off. I didn't feel stuck or glued to my chair at all. I marched right out to the garage, unlocked the padlock—which always sticks a bit when it's humid, I really need to get some graphite spray for that, remind me to write that down later—and I pulled the heavy bins right out into the center of the floor. I laid out my canvas tarp, got my three sorting boxes ready... I was completely ready to dive in. I initiated the whole process without a second thought. 

It's just... well, like I was saying before, it's what happens *after* I start that's the issue. I got everything laid out, picked up the first tool—an old Stanley block plane, looked like a number 60 and a half, a beautiful little tool if it were just cleaned up properly—and then... then the planning part I mentioned just fell apart on me. I couldn't figure out which box it should go in, or what I needed to do to restore it. 

But the actual *starting*? Getting up, putting myself in position to do the work? No, I don't have any trouble with that whatsoever. I'm not sitting around staring at the wall unable to move, if that's what you mean. I'm always trying to get going. I want to be doing these things. I just... I stall out once I'm actually standing there at the workbench. But getting to the workbench is no problem at all.
=====================================

It just vanishes. And then I'm sitting there staring at a blank piece of paper, trying to wrangle these thoughts back into order, but they just won't line up. It's like trying to hold water in your hands. You know it's there, you can feel it, but it just slips right through your fingers no matter how tightly you squeeze them together. \n\nI used to be able to juggle three different projects at once in the shop. I could have one group of kids sanding, another group measuring, and another group assembling, and I knew exactly where everyone was and what they were doing at any given moment. I could keep track of it all in my head without even breaking a sweat. But now? Now, just trying to figure out the steps to build a single birdhouse—a project I designed myself, mind you—it feels like trying to solve a complex algebraic equation in my head while someone is shouting at me. The individual tasks, like the cutting or the gluing, those are familiar, yes. But trying to string them together into a coherent plan? It's just... it's impossible. My brain just won't do it anymore. It just scatters everything to the wind.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it hard to start tasks or activities, even when you know you need to do them, or do you often feel stuck and unable to get going?\n\nPatient: Starting them? No, no, it's not a matter of not wanting to do them or being unable to get out of the chair. I have the drive to get things done, I really do. The starting part is actually quite easy for me. \n\nTake yesterday morning, for example. I woke up at 6:00 AM sharp, same as I have every day since I retired. I had my coffee—black, just one cup, because Mary always used to say too much caffeine made me jittery, though I think it was just her excuse to keep the rest of the pot for herself. Anyway, I knew I needed to sort through the new donation bins for the youth center. We get all sorts of things dropped off by well-meaning folks, you know, old hand planes that are rusted completely shut, chisels with the handles rotted away, and someone has to go through them and decide what's salvageable for the kids and what just belongs in the scrap heap. \n\nI didn't sit there putting it off. I didn't feel stuck or glued to my chair at all. I marched right out to the garage, unlocked the padlock—which always sticks a bit when it's humid, I really need to get some graphite spray for that, remind me to write that down later—and I pulled the heavy bins right out into the center of the floor. I laid out my canvas tarp, got my three sorting boxes ready... I was completely ready to dive in. I initiated the whole process without a second thought. \n\nIt's just... well, like I was saying before, it's what happens *after* I start that's the issue. I got everything laid out, picked up the first tool—an old Stanley block plane, looked like a number 60 and a half, a beautiful little tool if it were just cleaned up properly—and then... then the planning part I mentioned just fell apart on me. I couldn't figure out which box it should go in, or what I needed to do to restore it. \n\nBut the actual *starting*? Getting up, putting myself in position to do the work? No, I don't have any trouble with that whatsoever. I'm not sitting around staring at the wall unable to move, if that's what you mean. I'm always trying to get going. I want to be doing these things. I just... I stall out once I'm actually standing there at the workbench. But getting to the workbench is no problem at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Five or ten years ago? Oh, good heavens, no. No, no. Ten years ago I was drafting blueprints for the new community center addition, managing contractors, keeping track of every single permit and supply order in my head. Even just a month ago, I was sharp as a tack. I could run the whole shop, manage a dozen kids, and keep the entire lumber inventory organized without missing a beat. This isn't some slow, creeping decline that's been sneaking up on me over the years. I know people my age sometimes start to lose their edge gradually, they start forgetting where they put their keys or what day it is, but that is absolutely not what this is. 

This complete inability to organize my thoughts and plan things out... it's very recent. It has only been going on for about three weeks. Exactly three weeks, really. It started right after my older sister died. 

We were... well, we were incredibly close, you see. She was the one who actually bought me my first real set of woodworking tools when I was just a boy. She saved up her own money from her after-school job at the five-and-dime to buy me a beautiful set of Marples chisels in a wooden presentation box for my birthday. I still have them on my bench, actually. I keep them oiled and sharpened. Anyway, she passed away three weeks ago, and ever since I got that phone call... it's like someone just reached into my head and flipped the main breaker switch. The power to that part of my brain is just gone. Before that day, I was completely fine, planning projects, writing schedules, doing everything I've always done. But these last three weeks, ever since she's been gone... my mind is just a scattered mess. It was like night and day, doctor, truly. One day I'm perfectly fine, and the next, I can't even figure out how to put a block plane in a sorting bin.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF06.7, F02.80, F04, F01.50\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Your Name]. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Doctor. Well... I suppose I came in because... well, it’s my mind. My mind just will not hold onto things anymore, and it’s become quite frightening, to be perfectly honest with you. I’ve always been a sharp person, got my degree, always prided myself on being capable and organized. Even now that I'm not working, I try to stay involved, keep myself useful. I help out with this youth maker program in the community—it’s a good group, we teach the kids how to build things, work with their hands, do a bit of basic engineering. \n\nBut... I sat down the other day to write a simple note for the program. Just a basic outline, some measurements and materials for the kids. It's something I could do in my sleep before. They completely scattered. I couldn't hold the thought long enough to write it down. I just stared at the paper for I don't know how long, trying to force it to make sense, but it was like trying to catch water with my bare hands. It takes me forever to do the simplest things now because my brain just refuses to cooperate, and I just... I don't know what's happening to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble remembering things, or do you ever feel confused about time, place, or people around you?\n\nPatient: Memory? No... no, I wouldn't say it's my memory. And I'm certainly not confused about where I am or who people are. I mean, I know what day it is, I know I'm sitting here in your office. It's not like I'm losing my past or forgetting where I put my keys. \n\nIf you asked me right now what the exact dimensions of the birdhouses we build are, I could tell you without missing a beat—six inches by eight inches, quarter-inch plywood, assembled with brad nails. The information is all still in there. My memory is perfectly intact, I assure you. \n\nIt's... well, it's like I was saying before, it's the execution of it. The filing cabinet in my head is still perfectly organized, but it’s as if the drawer is jammed shut and I'm just pulling and pulling at the handle. I remember what I want to do, I know exactly what I'm trying to achieve, but my brain just won't process the steps to get there. So, no, I'm not forgetting things, and I'm not lost in time. I'm very much aware of my surroundings, perhaps too aware of how useless my brain is being right now. It just refuses to process the thoughts, even though the memories themselves are sitting right there, clear as day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble finding the right words when you speak, or difficulty understanding what others are saying to you?\n\nPatient: Finding words? No, no, I wouldn't say that at all. My vocabulary is... well, it's as robust as it ever was. When I'm actually in a conversation, the words come out just fine. \n\nFor instance, just last Tuesday, or maybe it was Wednesday—no, it was Tuesday, because that's when the lumber delivery comes in for the youth program—I was having quite a long chat with the delivery driver about the quality of the pine they've been sending us lately. It’s been awfully knotty, you see, and you can’t have the kids trying to drive brad nails through knots, it’s just asking for a splinter or a bent nail, and it ruins the whole birdhouse. Anyway, I was explaining all of this to him, quite clearly, articulating exactly what we needed for the next batch. I didn't stumble over my words, I didn't forget what a two-by-four was called, I didn't have to pause and search for the right terminology. \n\nAnd when he was explaining the supply chain issues they’ve been having at the mill, I understood him perfectly. I followed his whole explanation about the timber shortages and the shipping delays without any trouble whatsoever. Even with the kids at the program—and let me tell you, the slang they use these days is practically a foreign language—but I can still follow along, I understand what they're trying to communicate to me. \n\nSo, no, communicating with people, understanding them... that's not the problem. I can hold a conversation just fine, as you can probably tell. If you and I are sitting here talking, or if I'm listening to you, I understand every single word you're saying. The language part of my brain seems to be doing its job properly. It's just... it's when I'm left to my own devices, trying to organize my own thoughts into an action, like with that piece of paper I mentioned earlier, that everything just grinds to an absolute halt. But the talking and the listening? No, those are perfectly fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble performing tasks that require coordination, like tying your shoes, using tools, or writing by hand—things you’ve done many times before but now feel awkward or difficult to carry out?\n\nPatient: Coordination? Oh, no, not at all. My hands work just as well as they always have. I mean, if my physical coordination were failing me, I certainly wouldn't be anywhere near a band saw or a drill press, let alone teaching twelve-year-olds how to use them safely. \n\nJust Thursday—yes, Thursday afternoon when we had the shop open—I was showing one of the boys, young Tommy, how to properly hold a coping saw to get a nice, clean curve on a piece of wood. My hands were perfectly steady. I can still thread a tiny little set screw into a brass hinge without dropping it, and I can tie my boots every morning in a firm double knot just like I've done for fifty years. I've always been good with my hands, you see. I built my own back deck back in '98, did all the measurements, all the cuts myself, and even now, I can still swing a hammer and hit the nail dead on the head every single time. \n\nAnd the writing... you mentioned writing by hand. See, that's the truly frustrating part of all this! The physical act of holding the pen, the grip, the way my hand physically moves across the paper—that's all perfectly fine. My handwriting is still as neat and legible as it was when I took drafting classes in college. It's not my hand that's the problem, it's... well, it's the brain telling the hand *what* words to form. If you asked me right now to just draw a straight line, or copy a shape, or even trace a stencil, I could do it with absolute precision. My motor skills haven't deteriorated one bit. \n\nIt's really quite strange when you think about it. My body, my hands, my physical coordination... they are all perfectly willing and able to do the work. It's just that the engine driving them—the mind—has completely stalled out on me. So, no, using tools, tying shoes, the mechanics of writing... none of that feels awkward at all. It's just the thinking behind it that's gone completely sideways.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble planning out steps for a project or keeping track of multiple things at once, even if each individual task feels familiar?\n\nPatient: Yes, exactly. That... that is exactly it. It's the planning, the organizing of the thoughts. That's actually the whole reason I came in to see you in the first place. I just... my mind simply will not hold onto things anymore. \n\nLike I was trying to explain earlier with that piece of paper... I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program. Just a basic list of materials we need for the next project, or a schedule for the kids, something I used to be able to do in my sleep. I've done it a thousand times over the years. I know we need the plywood, I know we need the brad nails, I know we need the wood glue. But when I actually sit down at my desk, with the pen in my hand—and as I said, the hand works fine—but when I try to actually put the plan together on the page... it's like the numbers and the words just scatter on me. \n\nI'll think, \"Okay, we need three sheets of quarter-inch plywood,\" and then I'll try to think about how many boxes of nails that requires, and suddenly the plywood thought is gone. It just vanishes. And then I'm sitting there staring at a blank piece of paper, trying to wrangle these thoughts back into order, but they just won't line up. It's like trying to hold water in your hands. You know it's there, you can feel it, but it just slips right through your fingers no matter how tightly you squeeze them together. \n\nI used to be able to juggle three different projects at once in the shop. I could have one group of kids sanding, another group measuring, and another group assembling, and I knew exactly where everyone was and what they were doing at any given moment. I could keep track of it all in my head without even breaking a sweat. But now? Now, just trying to figure out the steps to build a single birdhouse—a project I designed myself, mind you—it feels like trying to solve a complex algebraic equation in my head while someone is shouting at me. The individual tasks, like the cutting or the gluing, those are familiar, yes. But trying to string them together into a coherent plan? It's just... it's impossible. My brain just won't do it anymore. It just scatters everything to the wind.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it hard to start tasks or activities, even when you know you need to do them, or do you often feel stuck and unable to get going?\n\nPatient: Starting them? No, no, it's not a matter of not wanting to do them or being unable to get out of the chair. I have the drive to get things done, I really do. The starting part is actually quite easy for me. \n\nTake yesterday morning, for example. I woke up at 6:00 AM sharp, same as I have every day since I retired. I had my coffee—black, just one cup, because Mary always used to say too much caffeine made me jittery, though I think it was just her excuse to keep the rest of the pot for herself. Anyway, I knew I needed to sort through the new donation bins for the youth center. We get all sorts of things dropped off by well-meaning folks, you know, old hand planes that are rusted completely shut, chisels with the handles rotted away, and someone has to go through them and decide what's salvageable for the kids and what just belongs in the scrap heap. \n\nI didn't sit there putting it off. I didn't feel stuck or glued to my chair at all. I marched right out to the garage, unlocked the padlock—which always sticks a bit when it's humid, I really need to get some graphite spray for that, remind me to write that down later—and I pulled the heavy bins right out into the center of the floor. I laid out my canvas tarp, got my three sorting boxes ready... I was completely ready to dive in. I initiated the whole process without a second thought. \n\nIt's just... well, like I was saying before, it's what happens *after* I start that's the issue. I got everything laid out, picked up the first tool—an old Stanley block plane, looked like a number 60 and a half, a beautiful little tool if it were just cleaned up properly—and then... then the planning part I mentioned just fell apart on me. I couldn't figure out which box it should go in, or what I needed to do to restore it. \n\nBut the actual *starting*? Getting up, putting myself in position to do the work? No, I don't have any trouble with that whatsoever. I'm not sitting around staring at the wall unable to move, if that's what you mean. I'm always trying to get going. I want to be doing these things. I just... I stall out once I'm actually standing there at the workbench. But getting to the workbench is no problem at all.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this difficulty with organizing your thoughts and planning tasks is a noticeable change from how you used to function, say, five or ten years ago?\n\nPatient: Five or ten years ago? Oh, good heavens, no. No, no. Ten years ago I was drafting blueprints for the new community center addition, managing contractors, keeping track of every single permit and supply order in my head. Even just a month ago, I was sharp as a tack. I could run the whole shop, manage a dozen kids, and keep the entire lumber inventory organized without missing a beat. This isn't some slow, creeping decline that's been sneaking up on me over the years. I know people my age sometimes start to lose their edge gradually, they start forgetting where they put their keys or what day it is, but that is absolutely not what this is. \n\nThis complete inability to organize my thoughts and plan things out... it's very recent. It has only been going on for about three weeks. Exactly three weeks, really. It started right after my older sister died. \n\nWe were... well, we were incredibly close, you see. She was the one who actually bought me my first real set of woodworking tools when I was just a boy. She saved up her own money from her after-school job at the five-and-dime to buy me a beautiful set of Marples chisels in a wooden presentation box for my birthday. I still have them on my bench, actually. I keep them oiled and sharpened. Anyway, she passed away three weeks ago, and ever since I got that phone call... it's like someone just reached into my head and flipped the main breaker switch. The power to that part of my brain is just gone. Before that day, I was completely fine, planning projects, writing schedules, doing everything I've always done. But these last three weeks, ever since she's been gone... my mind is just a scattered mess. It was like night and day, doctor, truly. One day I'm perfectly fine, and the next, I can't even figure out how to put a block plane in a sorting bin.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

  "note": "Ruled out F02.80 (Other dementia in other diseases classified elsewhere) due to absence of memory loss, confusion, or language comprehension issues. Patient retains memory and language function, but struggles specifically with executive functioning (planning, organizing thoughts), which is more consistent with disorders like F06.7 (Other specified mental disorders due to known physiological condition) or F04 (Organic amnestic syndrome, but not primarily memory-related). The acute onset and relation to grief may suggest a functional or stress-related organic basis.",

Now, just trying to figure out the steps to build a single birdhouse—a project I designed myself, mind you—it feels like trying to solve a complex algebraic equation in my head while someone is shouting at me. The individual tasks, like the cutting or the gluing, those are familiar, yes. But trying to string them together into a coherent plan? It's just... it's impossible. My brain just won't do it anymore. It just scatters everything to the wind.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it hard to start tasks or activities, even when you know you need to do them, or do you often feel stuck and unable to get going?\n\nPatient: Starting them? No, no, it's not a matter of not wanting to do them or being unable to get out of the chair. I have the drive to get things done, I really do. The starting part is actually quite easy for me. \n\nTake yesterday morning, for example. I woke up at 6:00 AM sharp, same as I have every day since I retired. I had my coffee—black, just one cup, because Mary always used to say too much caffeine made me jittery, though I think it was just her excuse to keep the rest of the pot for herself. Anyway, I knew I needed to sort through the new donation bins for the youth center. We get all sorts of things dropped off by well-meaning folks, you know, old hand planes that are rusted completely shut, chisels with the handles rotted away, and someone has to go through them and decide what's salvageable for the kids and what just belongs in the scrap heap. \n\nI didn't sit there putting it off. I didn't feel stuck or glued to my chair at all. I marched right out to the garage, unlocked the padlock—which always sticks a bit when it's humid, I really need to get some graphite spray for that, remind me to write that down later—and I pulled the heavy bins right out into the center of the floor. I laid out my canvas tarp, got my three sorting boxes ready... I was completely ready to dive in. I initiated the whole process without a second thought. \n\nIt's just... well, like I was saying before, it's what happens *after* I start that's the issue. I got everything laid out, picked up the first tool—an old Stanley block plane, looked like a number 60 and a half, a beautiful little tool if it were just cleaned up properly—and then... then the planning part I mentioned just fell apart on me. I couldn't figure out which box it should go in, or what I needed to do to restore it. \n\nBut the actual *starting*? Getting up, putting myself in position to do the work? No, I don't have any trouble with that whatsoever. I'm not sitting around staring at the wall unable to move, if that's what you mean. I'm always trying to get going. I want to be doing these things. I just... I stall out once I'm actually standing there at the workbench. But getting to the workbench is no problem at all.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this difficulty with organizing your thoughts and planning tasks is a noticeable change from how you used to function, say, five or ten years ago?\n\nPatient: Five or ten years ago? Oh, good heavens, no. No, no. Ten years ago I was drafting blueprints for the new community center addition, managing contractors, keeping track of every single permit and supply order in my head. Even just a month ago, I was sharp as a tack. I could run the whole shop, manage a dozen kids, and keep the entire lumber inventory organized without missing a beat. This isn't some slow, creeping decline that's been sneaking up on me over the years. I know people my age sometimes start to lose their edge gradually, they start forgetting where they put their keys or what day it is, but that is absolutely not what this is. \n\nThis complete inability to organize my thoughts and plan things out... it's very recent. It has only been going on for about three weeks. Exactly three weeks, really. It started right after my older sister died. \n\nWe were... well, we were incredibly close, you see. She was the one who actually bought me my first real set of woodworking tools when I was just a boy. She saved up her own money from her after-school job at the five-and-dime to buy me a beautiful set of Marples chisels in a wooden presentation box for my birthday. I still have them on my bench, actually. I keep them oiled and sharpened. Anyway, she passed away three weeks ago, and ever since I got that phone call... it's like someone just reached into my head and flipped the main breaker switch. The power to that part of my brain is just gone. Before that day, I was completely fine, planning projects, writing schedules, doing everything I've always done. But these last three weeks, ever since she's been gone... my mind is just a scattered mess. It was like night and day, doctor, truly. One day I'm perfectly fine, and the next, I can't even figure out how to put a block plane in a sorting bin.\n\n[Last Question]\nSince your sister passed away, have you been feeling down or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, like working with the kids in the maker program?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... yes. Yes, I have. It's... it's like the color has just drained out of everything, if that makes any sense. You mentioned the maker program, and... well, I have these beautiful pieces of kiln-dried black walnut just sitting right there on my main workbench. I hand-picked them from the lumber yard over in Westville just a few days before my sister passed. I was going to turn them into a set of matching bowls for the spring charity auction. Beautiful grain on them, truly, just waiting to be shaped. But since I got that phone call... I have not touched the wood pieces on my bench. Not once. I walk out to the garage, I look at them sitting there, and I just feel... nothing. No spark. No desire to turn on the lathe or pick up a gouge. I just turn around and walk back inside. 

And it isn’t just the woodworking, either. I’ve completely skipped out on the choir planning committee. We were supposed to be finalizing the hymns and the choral arrangements for the upcoming services, which is something I’ve helped organize for, oh, it must be fifteen years now. I've always loved music, my sister and I both did, we sang together in the youth choir back when we were kids. But I just didn't go to the meeting. I didn't even call them to say I wouldn't be there. I just let it go. 

Same with my chess games. I play—well, I *used* to play—every Thursday evening at the library with my friend Arthur. We use this lovely weighted Staunton set I restored for him a few years back. But I’ve skipped chess every single week since she died. Arthur called twice to check on me, bless his heart, but I just can't bring myself to care about moving pieces around a checkered board right now. It all just feels so hollow. 

Even the fishing... I usually love getting out on the water. I have a little aluminum rowboat, nothing fancy, but I love getting out there at dawn when the mist is still coming off the lake. It's always been my quiet time. But now? Even the thought of hitching up the trailer, packing the tackle box, casting a line... even fishing sounds like more effort than it is worth. It feels like climbing a mountain just to sit in a boat. 

And that is really not like me at all, doctor. I’ve always been a man who keeps busy, who loves his hobbies, who takes pride in his commitments to the community. I was never one to just sit around the house. But lately, I just... I don't care about any of it. The joy is just completely gone.

I'm Dr. [Your Name]. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Doctor. Well... I suppose I came in because... well, it’s my mind. My mind just will not hold onto things anymore, and it’s become quite frightening, to be perfectly honest with you. I’ve always been a sharp person, got my degree, always prided myself on being capable and organized. Even now that I'm not working, I try to stay involved, keep myself useful. I help out with this youth maker program in the community—it’s a good group, we teach the kids how to build things, work with their hands, do a bit of basic engineering. \n\nBut... I sat down the other day to write a simple note for the program. Just a basic outline, some measurements and materials for the kids. It's something I could do in my sleep before. I mean, I’ve done it a thousand times over the years without a second thought. But I sat there at my desk, pen in hand, and... the numbers and the words, they just scattered on me. They completely scattered. I couldn't hold the thought long enough to write it down. I just stared at the paper for I don't know how long, trying to force it to make sense, but it was like trying to catch water with my bare hands. It takes me forever to do the simplest things now because my brain just refuses to cooperate, and I just... I don't know what's happening to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble remembering things, or do you ever feel confused about time, place, or people around you?\n\nPatient: Memory? No... no, I wouldn't say it's my memory. And I'm certainly not confused about where I am or who people are. I mean, I know what day it is, I know I'm sitting here in your office. I know my neighbors' names, I know all the kids at the maker program. It's not like that at all. It's not like I'm losing my past or forgetting where I put my keys. \n\nIf you asked me right now what the exact dimensions of the birdhouses we build are, I could tell you without missing a beat—six inches by eight inches, quarter-inch plywood, assembled with brad nails. The information is all still in there. My memory is perfectly intact, I assure you. \n\nIt's... well, it's like I was saying before, it's the execution of it. The filing cabinet in my head is still perfectly organized, but it’s as if the drawer is jammed shut and I'm just pulling and pulling at the handle. I remember what I want to do, I know exactly what I'm trying to achieve, but my brain just won't process the steps to get there. So, no, I'm not forgetting things, and I'm not lost in time. I'm very much aware of my surroundings, perhaps too aware of how useless my brain is being right now. It just refuses to process the thoughts, even though the memories themselves are sitting right there, clear as day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble finding the right words when you speak, or difficulty understanding what others are saying to you?\n\nPatient: Finding words? Anyway, I was explaining all of this to him, quite clearly, articulating exactly what we needed for the next batch. I didn't stumble over my words, I didn't forget what a two-by-four was called, I didn't have to pause and search for the right terminology. \n\nAnd when he was explaining the supply chain issues they’ve been having at the mill, I understood him perfectly. I followed his whole explanation about the timber shortages and the shipping delays without any trouble whatsoever. I've always been good with my hands, you see. I built my own back deck back in '98, did all the measurements, all the cuts myself, and even now, I can still swing a hammer and hit the nail dead on the head every single time. \n\nAnd the writing... you mentioned writing by hand. She saved up her own money from her after-school job at the five-and-dime to buy me a beautiful set of Marples chisels in a wooden presentation box for my birthday. I still have them on my bench, actually. I keep them oiled and sharpened. Anyway, she passed away three weeks ago, and ever since I got that phone call... it's like someone just reached into my head and flipped the main breaker switch. The power to that part of my brain is just gone. Before that day, I was completely fine, planning projects, writing schedules, doing everything I've always done. But these last three weeks, ever since she's been gone... my mind is just a scattered mess. It was like night and day, doctor, truly. One day I'm perfectly fine, and the next, I can't even figure out how to put a block plane in a sorting bin.\n\nDoctor: Since your sister passed away, have you been feeling down or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, like working with the kids in the maker program?\n\nPatient: Well... yes. Yes, I have. It's... it's like the color has just drained out of everything, if that makes any sense. You mentioned the maker program, and... well, I have these beautiful pieces of kiln-dried black walnut just sitting right there on my main workbench. I hand-picked them from the lumber yard over in Westville just a few days before my sister passed. I was going to turn them into a set of matching bowls for the spring charity auction. Beautiful grain on them, truly, just waiting to be shaped. But since I got that phone call... I have not touched the wood pieces on my bench. Not once. I walk out to the garage, I look at them sitting there, and I just feel... nothing. No spark. No desire to turn on the lathe or pick up a gouge. I just turn around and walk back inside. \n\nAnd it isn’t just the woodworking, either. I’ve completely skipped out on the choir planning committee. We were supposed to be finalizing the hymns and the choral arrangements for the upcoming services, which is something I’ve helped organize for, oh, it must be fifteen years now. I've always loved music, my sister and I both did, we sang together in the youth choir back when we were kids. But I just didn't go to the meeting. I didn't even call them to say I wouldn't be there. I just let it go. \n\nSame with my chess games. I play—well, I *used* to play—every Thursday evening at the library with my friend Arthur. We use this lovely weighted Staunton set I restored for him a few years back. But I’ve skipped chess every single week since she died. Arthur called twice to check on me, bless his heart, but I just can't bring myself to care about moving pieces around a checkered board right now. It all just feels so hollow. \n\nEven the fishing... I usually love getting out on the water. I have a little aluminum rowboat, nothing fancy, but I love getting out there at dawn when the mist is still coming off the lake. It's always been my quiet time. But now? Even the thought of hitching up the trailer, packing the tackle box, casting a line... even fishing sounds like more effort than it is worth. It feels like climbing a mountain just to sit in a boat. \n\nAnd that is really not like me at all, doctor. I’ve always been a man who keeps busy, who loves his hobbies, who takes pride in his commitments to the community. I was never one to just sit around the house. But lately, I just... I don't care about any of it. The joy is just completely gone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes... yes, that's exactly it. It's not just when I'm looking at her old photographs or actively thinking about the funeral or how much I miss her. It's... it's a constant thing. 

Most days, I wake up—like I mentioned earlier, right at six o'clock—and before I even have a chance to remember that she's gone, before my brain even really turns over for the day, there's already this heavy, hollow feeling sitting right in the middle of my chest. It's almost like a physical weight, you know? Like someone poured a bag of wet concrete right on top of my ribs while I was sleeping, and it just settled there, thick and immovable. 

And it just stays there all day. It really doesn't matter what's going on around me, either. My wife, Mary... she's been so incredibly patient with me through all of this, an absolute saint, really. She'll come into the kitchen and sit down at the breakfast table with me, and she'll be talking so kindly, maybe telling me about her garden club or complaining about the neighbor's golden retriever digging under the azaleas again—just normal, everyday things, trying to keep the morning pleasant and normal. 

But even then, even when she's right there, speaking to me with so much warmth and trying her best to cheer me up... I just feel like I'm sitting at the very bottom of a deep, dark well. I can hear her voice, I know she's up there in the light, but I'm just stuck down in the cold and the dark, and I can't reach her. I can't feel any of that warmth. The sadness is just always there, completely surrounding me, whether I'm actively grieving my sister or just trying to drink my morning coffee. It just doesn't lift, doctor. Not even for a minute.

I’ve always been a sharp person, got my degree, always prided myself on being capable and organized. Even now that I'm not working, I try to stay involved, keep myself useful. I mean, I’ve done it a thousand times over the years without a second thought. But I sat there at my desk, pen in hand, and... the numbers and the words, they just scattered on me. They completely scattered. I couldn't hold the thought long enough to write it down. I just stared at the paper for I don't know how long, trying to force it to make sense, but it was like trying to catch water with my bare hands. It takes me forever to do the simplest things now because my brain just refuses to cooperate, and I just... I don't know what's happening to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble remembering things, or do you ever feel confused about time, place, or people around you?\n\nPatient: Memory? It's not like I'm losing my past or forgetting where I put my keys. \n\nIf you asked me right now what the exact dimensions of the birdhouses we build are, I could tell you without missing a beat—six inches by eight inches, quarter-inch plywood, assembled with brad nails. The information is all still in there. My memory is perfectly intact, I assure you. \n\nIt's... well, it's like I was saying before, it's the execution of it. The filing cabinet in my head is still perfectly organized, but it’s as if the drawer is jammed shut and I'm just pulling and pulling at the handle. I'm very much aware of my surroundings, perhaps too aware of how useless my brain is being right now. It just refuses to process the thoughts, even though the memories themselves are sitting right there, clear as day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble finding the right words when you speak, or difficulty understanding what others are saying to you?\n\nPatient: Finding words? No, no, I wouldn't say that at all. My vocabulary is... well, it's as robust as it ever was. Anyway, I was explaining all of this to him, quite clearly, articulating exactly what we needed for the next batch. I didn't stumble over my words, I didn't forget what a two-by-four was called, I didn't have to pause and search for the right terminology. \n\nAnd when he was explaining the supply chain issues they’ve been having at the mill, I understood him perfectly. I can hold a conversation just fine, as you can probably tell. If you and I are sitting here talking, or if I'm listening to you, I understand every single word you're saying. Oh, no, not at all. My hands work just as well as they always have. I mean, if my physical coordination were failing me, I certainly wouldn't be anywhere near a band saw or a drill press, let alone teaching twelve-year-olds how to use them safely. \n\nJust Thursday—yes, Thursday afternoon when we had the shop open—I was showing one of the boys, young Tommy, how to properly hold a coping saw to get a nice, clean curve on a piece of wood. My hands were perfectly steady. I can still thread a tiny little set screw into a brass hinge without dropping it, and I can tie my boots every morning in a firm double knot just like I've done for fifty years. I've always been good with my hands, you see. I built my own back deck back in '98, did all the measurements, all the cuts myself, and even now, I can still swing a hammer and hit the nail dead on the head every single time. \n\nAnd the writing... you mentioned writing by hand. See, that's the truly frustrating part of all this! The physical act of holding the pen, the grip, the way my hand physically moves across the paper—that's all perfectly fine. I can hear her voice, I know she's up there in the light, but I'm just stuck down in the cold and the dark, and I can't reach her. I can't feel any of that warmth. The sadness is just always there, completely surrounding me, whether I'm actively grieving my sister or just trying to drink my morning coffee. It just doesn't lift, doctor. Not even for a minute.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

These symptoms suggest a major depressive episode (F32.2) in addition to the previous differential of dementia (F01.50), amnestic disorder (F04), and mild neurocognitive disorder (F06.7). ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sleep? No, no, my sleep is actually... well, it's one of the few things that hasn't changed, thankfully. 

I know I mentioned waking up at six o'clock earlier, but I should probably clarify—that is absolutely not me waking up earlier than I'd like. That is just my normal time. I have woken up at exactly six o'clock in the morning, give or take a few minutes, for the better part of forty years. Even back when I was working, long before I retired, I always liked to be up before the sun. I'd go down to the kitchen, grind the coffee beans—Mary got me one of those fancy burr grinders for our anniversary a few years back, and let me tell you, it makes a world of difference with the flavor, truly—and I'd just sit and read the paper in the quiet. 

So, no, I'm not waking up early because of... well, because of everything that's happened. And I sleep right through the night, too. We have this wonderful firm mattress, an orthopedic one we bought from that big furniture showroom out on Route 9, because my lower back used to give me a bit of trouble if I stood at the workbench too long. Ever since we got that mattress, I sleep like an absolute log. 

I usually turn in around ten o'clock, right after the evening news finishes. Mary likes to stay up a bit later reading her mystery novels—she has this little LED reading lamp she clips to her book so the light doesn't bother me—but honestly, once my head hits that pillow, I am out. I don't toss, I don't turn, I don't wake up in the middle of the night staring at the ceiling. I just close my eyes at ten, and the next thing I know, it's six in the morning. 

It's just... like I was saying before, it's what happens *after* I wake up that's the problem. That heavy feeling sets in right away. But the actual sleeping part? No, that's perfectly fine. Completely uninterrupted, really. I suppose I'm lucky in that regard, because I know some folks my age who are up three or four times a night, pacing the halls or going to the kitchen for a glass of warm milk, but not me. I sleep just fine.
